**Indonesia**,[^cite_note-13] officially the **Republic of Indonesia**,[^cite_note-fn1-15] is a country in [Southeast Asia](28741.md) and [Oceania](22621.md), between the [Indian](14580.md) and [Pacific](23070.md) oceans. Comprising between [13,000 and 17,000 islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Indonesia), including [Sumatra](28679.md), [Java](69336.md), [Sulawesi](28740.md), and parts of [Borneo](4518.md) and [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea), it is the world's largest [archipelagic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelagic_state) and the [14th-largest country by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area). Indonesia has extensive tropical forests and marine ecosystems that support one of the [world's highest levels of biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megadiverse_countries).&#x20;

The [Indonesian archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusantara_\(term\)) has been inhabited since prehistoric times, with early human presence evidenced by fossils of *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)* and *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens)*, and megalithic sites. It became connected to regional and international maritime trade networks by the first millennium BCE. [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Indonesia) and [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Indonesia) kingdoms developed by early centuries CE, followed by the [spread of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam_in_Indonesia) and the growth of Islamic states from the late medieval period. European powers later competed to monopolise trade in the Spice Islands of [Maluku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands) during the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery). Dutch commercial and colonial control expanded from the 17th century and was consolidated over most of the archipelago during the 19th and early 20th centuries, before Indonesia [proclaimed its independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Indonesian_Independence) in the [aftermath of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_World_War_II).&#x20;

Since independence, Indonesia has experienced [separatist conflicts](286527.md), corruption, political upheaval and natural disasters, alongside [democratisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Suharto_era_in_Indonesia) and rapid economic growth. The country today is a [presidential republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_system) with an elected [legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Consultative_Assembly) and consists of [38 provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Indonesia), some of which enjoy [greater autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_administrative_division) than others. Home to over 280 million people, Indonesia [ranks fourth in the world by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) and has the largest [Muslim population of any country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_by_country). More than half of Indonesians live on [Java](69336.md), the [most heavily populated island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_by_population) in the world, while the capital [Jakarta](16275.md) is the world's [most populous city](14649921.md).&#x20;

Indonesia has hundreds of [ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Indonesia) and more than 700 [languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Indonesia), alongside diverse regional cultures. This diversity is reflected in the national motto, *[Bhinneka Tunggal Ika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhinneka_Tunggal_Ika)*, meaning "unity in diversity". [Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language), a standardised variety of [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language), is the official and national language and its *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)*. Indonesia has the largest [economy](14647.md) in Southeast Asia, with services and industry accounting for most output and economic activity concentrated on Java. Sea and air transport are the primary means of connecting the country's many islands. Indonesia is a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) active in regional and global affairs and is a member of the [United Nations](31769.md), [G20](2206608.md), the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement), [ASEAN](106539.md), and the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md).&#x20;

## Etymology

Main article: [Names of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Indonesia)

The name *Indonesia* is usually explained as a combination of *India* and the [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) word *[nesos](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%BD%E1%BF%86%CF%83%CE%BF%CF%82)*, together meaning 'Indian islands'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown20042-16] In 1850, [George Windsor Earl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Windsor_Earl), an English [ethnologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnology), proposed the terms *Indunesians* and *Malayunesians* for the inhabitants of the "Indian Archipelago or [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago)", preferring the latter because he considered *Indunesians* too general.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEarl185071-17][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElson20081-18] In the same journal, [James Richardson Logan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Richardson_Logan) adopted *Indonesia* as a geographical term for the *Indian Archipelago*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELogan1850254-19][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElson20081–2-20]&#x20;

Usage remained limited for several decades. German ethnographer [Adolf Bastian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Bastian) from the [University of Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humboldt_University_of_Berlin) gave the term greater scholarly currency by using it in the title of his five-volume work *Indonesia, or the Islands of the Malay Archipelago* ([German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language): *Indonesien oder die Inseln des Malayischen Archipel*), published from 1884 to 1894.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElson20082-21] Dutch scholars nevertheless tended to use *Malay Archipelago* ([Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language): *Maleische Archipel*) or the *[Netherlands East Indies](23476997.md)* (*Nederlandsch Oost Indië*). Popular alternatives included *Indië*, *de Oost* ("the East"), and, less often, *Insulinde*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_der_Kroef1951167–168-22]&#x20;

After 1900, *Indonesia* became increasingly common in academic and public usage, and Indonesian nationalist organisations adopted it as an expression of national unity and freedom from colonial rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown20042-16] Among its early Indonesian promoters was [Soewardi Soerjaningrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ki_Hajar_Dewantara) (Ki Hajar Dewantara), who established the *Indonesisch Persbureau* in [The Hague](30269.md) in November 1918, a press bureau that used *Indonesia* in its name.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Thanks_to_Soewardi,_the_Name_'Indonesia'_Took_Root_in_The_Hague_in_1918"._National_Geographic_Indonesia,_2024-23]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Indonesia)

### Prehistory, early states, and Islamisation

Main articles: [Prehistoric Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Indonesia) and [Spread of Islam in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam_in_Indonesia)

Reddish ochre outline of a large animal painted on a rough limestone cave wall.

A prehistoric [animal figure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Figurative_art) in the *[Lubang Jeriji Saléh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lubang_Jeriji_Sal%C3%A9h)* cave, estimated to be more than 40,000 years old.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Oldest_example_of_figurative_art_found_in_Indonesian_cave"._The_Art_Newspaper,_2024-24]

The island of [Java](69336.md) has yielded numerous hominin fossils generally attributed to *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)*, including the type specimen discovered by [Eugene Dubois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugene_Dubois) at [Trinil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinil).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPope198844,_50,_59–60-25][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_VosSondaar19941726-26] Stone artefacts on [Flores](11426.md) show that hominins had reached the island by at least 1.02 million years ago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrummJensenvan_den_BerghMorwood2010748-27] *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens)* had dispersed into Southeast Asia by at least 50,000 years ago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'ConnorBulbeck2013346-28] In [Sulawesi](28740.md), narrative cave art has been dated to a minimum age of 51,200 years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Oldest_example_of_figurative_art_found_in_Indonesian_cave"._The_Art_Newspaper,_2024-24][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOktavianaJoannes-BoyauHakim2024814-29] Flores also yielded remains of the small-bodied hominin *[Homo floresiensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_floresiensis)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorwood20051012-30] Megalithic traditions later developed at different times across the archipelago, and stone monuments were used for burial and to honour or commemorate ancestors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESteimer-Herbet2018vii,_1-31]&#x20;

Beginning in the third millennium BCE, [Austronesian-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages) communities moved south from Taiwan through the Philippines and into the Indonesian archipelago, where related languages eventually spread across most of the islands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor20036–7-32][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBellwoodFoxTryon20061,_4-33] Settlement and subsistence patterns varied across the islands, with communities combining fishing, hunting, gathering, and cultivation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor20037–9-34] From around 500 BCE, regional trading networks increasingly connected the archipelago with [South](21566765.md) and [East Asia](19605700.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor200310,_15–16-35] Imported goods, knowledge, and technologies circulated through these networks, and during the early centuries CE some coastal communities in [Borneo](4518.md), [Sumatra](28679.md), and Java developed into trading ports.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall201139–40-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor200315–18-37] Indian merchants and religious specialists brought [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) literacy, [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Indonesia) and [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Indonesia) practices, and models of kingship that local rulers adopted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor200318–22-38][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERahman201457–58-39]&#x20;

[Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya), founded in southeastern Sumatra in the late 7th century CE, grew as a maritime trading polity and a centre of Buddhist learning and religious networks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEManguin202187,_92–94-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor200324–25-41] In central Java, rulers supported the construction of major Hindu and Buddhist monuments between the 8th and 10th centuries, including [Borobudur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borobudur) and [Prambanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prambanan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunandar2017661–663-42] [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit) emerged in eastern Java in the late 13th century after [Raden Wijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raden_Wijaya) used a [Mongol expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Java) to defeat his rivals and then forced the Mongols to withdraw.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2011257-43] Its rulers claimed suzerainty over a wide network of ports across the archipelago, although beyond Java this authority was often exercised indirectly through tributary, ritual, and commercial relationships rather than continuous territorial control.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2011256–258-44]&#x20;

By the late 13th century, Muslim rulers were established in parts of northern Sumatra. Perlak was described by [Marco Polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marco_Polo) as Muslim in 1292, and a royal tomb at Pasai dated 1297 records a Muslim ruler there.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown200431-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs20014-46] [Samudra-Pasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samudera_Pasai_Sultanate) became an important pepper port and a major centre for Islamic learning.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2011288-47] [Islam spread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam_in_Indonesia) further through maritime trading networks, with Muslim communities becoming established at ports along Java's north coast and elsewhere in the archipelago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown200432-48][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor200376-49] Majapahit declined during the 15th century, and [Demak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demak_Sultanate) emerged as an independent north-coast sultanate and an important centre of Javanese Islam.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2011281–286-50] Early conversions were associated with the *[Wali Sanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali_Sanga)*, the nine saints traditionally credited with the spread of Islam in Java.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeener2010475-51] Islamic practice there also incorporated elements of older Javanese culture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs20018–9-52]&#x20;

### European contact and colonial rule

Main articles: [Dutch East India Company in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company_in_Indonesia) and [Dutch East Indies](23476997.md)

Diponegoro stands before Dutch officers outside a colonial building as several Javanese followers kneel nearby.

An 1835 painting of the submission of Prince [Diponegoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diponegoro) to [General De Kock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hendrik_Merkus_de_Kock) at the end of the [Java War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java_War) in 1830

Before European arrival, spices from [Maluku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands) were already traded through Asian maritime networks in which Javanese and other regional merchants participated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown200430-53][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2011287-54] The Portuguese [captured Malacca in 1511](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Malacca_\(1511\)) and reached Maluku the following year, where they sought control of the spice trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown200433-55] [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic) expeditions followed in the 1590s, and in 1602 competing Dutch companies were merged into the [Dutch East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company) (*Vereenigde Oost-Indische Compagnie*, VOC). Its charter authorised it to wage war, build fortresses, and conclude treaties in Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs200131-56] In 1619, the VOC [captured Jayakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Jayakarta) (present-day [Jakarta](16275.md)), renamed it [Batavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavia,_Dutch_East_Indies), and turned it into its headquarters. It developed as an administrative and commercial centre and attracted Chinese migrants and others from the archipelago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2003146–147-57][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs200135-58] The VOC was dissolved in 1800, and its possessions were passed to the [Dutch state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavian_Republic).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001144-59]&#x20;

During the 19th century, Dutch power expanded across the archipelago and met sustained resistance in [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java_War), [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padri_War), [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_intervention_in_Bali_\(1908\)), and [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200321-60] In [western New Guinea](1432822.md), the Dutch established an outpost in 1828, but abandoned it in 1836 and began permanent occupation only after 1898.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001178-61] By the early 20th century, military campaigns and boundary agreements had established Dutch control over a territory that largely corresponded to present-day Indonesia, known then as the [Netherlands Indies](23476997.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004102-62] The colonial administration ruled several territories directly and governed others through local states whose rulers retained limited authority under Dutch supremacy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004102-62]&#x20;

The [Ethical Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Ethical_Policy) became an official programme in 1901 and expanded vernacular schooling, irrigation, and other public services.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2003284-63] Western-style schooling also expanded, but it reached only a small minority of the population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2003285–286-64] During this period, several political organisations were founded, including [Budi Utomo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budi_Utomo) in 1908 and [Sarekat Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarekat_Islam) in 1912,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004117,_119-65] followed by the [Communist Party of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Indonesia) (PKI) in 1920 and the [Indonesian National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Party) (PNI) in 1927.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200325–26-66] The [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) reached the colony in the early 1930s, bringing severe economic hardship and increasing fears among colonial authorities that discontent could strengthen radical nationalist movements. Restrictions on nationalist activity became stricter, and by the mid-1930s, [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno), [Mohammad Hatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Hatta), [Sutan Sjahrir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutan_Sjahrir), and other non-cooperative nationalist leaders had been imprisoned or exiled.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004132–133-67]&#x20;

### Emergence of Indonesia and independence

Main articles: [Indonesian National Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Awakening) and [Indonesian National Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Revolution)

Indonesian nationalists ride through a street with arms raised around a "Boeng, Ajo Boeng" poster.

Indonesian nationalists riding through the streets, November 1945

A shared national identity began developing through youth associations and a growing literature in the [Malay language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language), which had long been a *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)* that did not favour any single ethnic group. Nationalists increasingly called the language *[Bahasa Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language)* and made it a common medium for the emerging nation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001232–233-68] At the Second Youth Congress in Batavia in October 1928, delegates pledged allegiance to one homeland and one nation, Indonesia, and recognised [Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language) as the "language of unity".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004106–107-69][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001233-70]&#x20;

Dutch colonial rule collapsed when [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan) [conquered the colony in 1942](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_Indies_campaign).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001244-71] The [occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Dutch_East_Indies) imposed martial law, banned political parties, and mobilised Indonesians through Japanese-sponsored civil and quasi-military organisations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004140-72] Wartime shortages and forced labour caused widespread hardship, but the ban on the [Dutch language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) strengthened the public use of Indonesian.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200334-73][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004141-74] Japanese-sponsored forces such as [PETA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenders_of_the_Homeland) and the Sumatran *Giyugun* trained tens of thousands of Indonesians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004146-75] A day after the [atomic bombing of Hiroshima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki), Japanese authorities permitted Indonesian leaders to begin preparing institutions and a constitution for an independent state planned for September 1945.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2003322-76]&#x20;

However, [Japan surrendered to the Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrender_of_Japan) on 15 August 1945. Two days later, [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno) [proclaimed Indonesian independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Indonesian_Independence), with Hatta co-signing the declaration.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2003323-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200331-78] They were named president and vice-president on 18 August.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2003325-79] Dutch attempts to restore colonial rule led to a four-year [military and diplomatic struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Revolution).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200335-80] International opposition to the Netherlands intensified after [its offensive of December 1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Kraai), and the [United States](3434750.md) subsequently pressed the Dutch into negotiations recommended by the [United Nations](31769.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200337–38-81] On 27 December 1949, the Netherlands [transferred sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch%E2%80%93Indonesian_Round_Table_Conference) to the federal [United States of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_of_Indonesia), except over western New Guinea.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001284-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200338-83] The federal states soon dissolved, and a unitary republic was proclaimed on 17 August 1950.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200338-83]&#x20;

### Sukarno era and Suharto's New Order

Main articles: [Liberal democracy period in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy_period_in_Indonesia), [Guided Democracy in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guided_Democracy_in_Indonesia), and [New Order (Indonesia)](2864794.md)

Formal black-and-white portrait of Sukarno wearing a white uniform and a black peci.

Formal portrait of Suharto wearing a decorated uniform with a ceremonial sash and medals sitting before the Indonesian flag and Garuda emblem.

[Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno) (*left*) and [Suharto](217513.md) (*right*), Indonesia's first two [presidents](10011631.md)

Indonesia adopted parliamentary government under the [1950 provisional constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Constitution_of_1950).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013148-84] The [1955 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Indonesian_legislative_election) produced no durable governing majority and did not end the persistent political crisis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESulistyo200275–76-85] In February 1958, dissidents in [West Sumatra](453590.md) proclaimed [its own government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Government_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia) before being joined by [rebels in Sulawesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permesta). They were seeking reforms to the republic rather than separation, but were defeated by the central government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2004188-86] After the [Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Assembly_of_Indonesia) failed to secure the required majority for a permanent constitution, Sukarno [dissolved it by decree in 1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_Sukarno%27s_1959_Decree) and restored the 1945 Constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend200368-87] This established the [Guided Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guided_Democracy_in_Indonesia), a system characterised by dominant presidential authority, less regional autonomy and reduced role for political parties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013148–149-88]&#x20;

Under this new system, Sukarno expanded the military's political role while supporting the growing PKI as a counterweight.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013148-84] His campaign against Dutch rule in [western New Guinea](1432822.md) led to the [New York Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Agreement) in 1962 and Indonesian administration of the territory in 1963.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Agreement_between_the_Republic_of_Indonesia_and_the_Kingdom_of_the_Netherlands_Concerning_West_New_Guinea_(New_York_Agreement)"._United_Nations_Department_of_Political_and_Peacebuilding_Affairs,_1962-89][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001328-90] After the [30 September Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_September_Movement) killed six generals in 1965, army general [Suharto](217513.md)'s forces took control of Jakarta and the military presented the movement as a PKI coup.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013161-91][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMelvin20181-92] The ensuing [anti-communist campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_mass_killings_of_1965%E2%80%9366) spread across several regions, with army units working alongside or directing civilian groups.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013162-93][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMelvin20181-92] At least 500,000 people were killed and hundreds of thousands were detained.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013164-98][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarid20053-99] The campaign destroyed the PKI and shifted power to the army.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELegge196841-100] The *[Supersemar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersemar)* order in 1966 gave Suharto *de facto* control over key executive and security powers, an effective transfer of power from Sukarno.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001349-101]&#x20;

Suharto's [New Order](2864794.md) restricted political organisation, embedded the military in government, and used his political party [Golkar](408234.md) and a tightly controlled party system to dominate elections.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013166–167-102] The [Act of Free Choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Free_Choice) in 1969 formally incorporated western New Guinea into Indonesia. However, just over a thousand selected community leaders took part in it amid reports of military intimidation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013168-103] Many Papuans opposed this integration and have waged an [armed resistance](21657843.md) since then.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEViartasiwi2018142-104][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChauvel2021917-105] In 1975, Indonesia [invaded Portuguese Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_invasion_of_East_Timor) and [annexed the territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor_\(province\)) the following year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001369–370-106] In Aceh, [Hasan di Tiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_di_Tiro) founded the [Free Aceh Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Aceh_Movement) (GAM) in 1976 and declared independence from Indonesia. The [insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency_in_Aceh) was largely suppressed by 1979, but revived in 1989 after the return of fighters trained in [Libya](17633.md), prompting a decade-long military campaign.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarronRahmanNugroho201310–11-107]&#x20;

The New Order reopened the economy to foreign capital and pursued industrialisation through investment, foreign aid, and state development plans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013168–169-108] Manufacturing expanded and the number of people below the official poverty line fell substantially by the mid-1990s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013205-109] At the same time, conglomerates connected to Suharto's family and close associates accumulated wealth through state patronage and political protection.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackie201091-110][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013207-111] The [Asian financial crisis of 1997–98](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis) caused a sharp economic downturn,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers2013209-112][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPincusRamli1998723-113] followed by protests and [unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_1998_Indonesia_riots) in several cities, including Jakarta, [Medan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medan), and [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta). This culminated in [Suharto's resignation in May 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Suharto).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicklefs2001404–407-114] In a [UN-supervised referendum in 1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_East_Timorese_independence_referendum), a majority of people in East Timor voted against remaining part Indonesia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend2003442-115] East Timor later became an independent country in 2002.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriend2003458-116][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"East_Timor_celebrates_becoming_a_nation"._The_Guardian,_2002-117]&#x20;

### *Reformasi* and contemporary Indonesia

Main article: [Reform era in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_era_in_Indonesia)

Aerial view of flattened buildings, debris and damaged coastline in Banda Aceh after the tsunami.

Damage following the [earthquake and subsequent tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_Ocean_earthquake_and_tsunami) in [Banda Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_Aceh), January 2005

The [period after Suharto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_era_in_Indonesia), known as *Reformasi* in Indonesia, brought constitutional amendments, competitive elections, and decentralisation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButt20101-118][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDagg200747,_49-119] The military lost its reserved parliamentary seats and the police became a separate service, but military influence did not disappear.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENalle2026132-120] Democratic institutions were established, but patronage and influence of oligarchy persisted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFukuoka2014548–550-121] Former general [Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susilo_Bambang_Yudhoyono) won the first direct [presidential election in 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indonesian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDagg200758-122] His administration reached a [peace agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Agreement) with Aceh separatists in 2005 after the previous year's [Indian Ocean tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_Ocean_earthquake_and_tsunami) brought more international attention to the province.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarronRahmanNugroho20131-123] Sporadic [bombings](9751508.md) occurred throughout the 2000s but had declined by the early 2010s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHwang20121–3-124]&#x20;

[Joko Widodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joko_Widodo), popularly called Jokowi, was elected president in [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indonesian_presidential_election) and prioritised infrastructure and economic development, which he linked to improving welfare.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Joko_Widodo's_mixed_legacy:_infrastructure_triumphs_and_democratic_concerns"._South_China_Morning_Post,_2024-125][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMietzner2025369–370-126] Indonesia remained an electoral democracy, but the quality of its democracy declined amid weakened parliamentary opposition and oversight, curbs on dissent, reduced independence of the [anti-corruption commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Eradication_Commission), and Jokowi's intervention in the [2024 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indonesian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadipalapaNainggolanKatharina2026267–268-127][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMietzner2025367-128] Under his successor [Prabowo Subianto](2829084.md), the government pursued a fiscally expansive programme,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWihardjaIkhsanAlatas2025169-129] and revised a military law in 2025, which allowed more active officers to serve in civilian posts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENalle2026125-130]&#x20;

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Indonesia) and [List of islands of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Indonesia)

Satellite view of the Indonesian archipelago, showing Sumatra, Java, Borneo, Sulawesi, the Lesser Sunda Islands, the Maluku Islands, and western New Guinea surrounded by blue seas.

A satellite image of the Indonesian archipelago.

Blue waters of Lake Toba winding between steep green hills viewed from above.

Indonesia lies within one of the most tectonically active regions on Earth.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECummins20171-131]

&#x20;Pictured above is [Lake Toba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Toba) in [North Sumatra](453595.md), the world's largest known [Cenozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenozoic) [caldera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caldera).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoulakov20091121-132]

Indonesia is an equatorial archipelago of coastal plains, mountains, and volcanic islands. It lies between latitudes [11°S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11th_parallel_south) and [6°N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_parallel_north) and longitudes [95°E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/95th_meridian_east) and [141°E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/141st_meridian_east),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParamitaMatzarakis2023283-133] and is the world's largest [archipelagic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelagic_state), extending 5,120 kilometres (3,181 mi) from east to west and 1,760 kilometres (1,094 mi) from north to south.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrederickWorden201198-134] Several straits, including [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Strait), [Sunda](177179.md), and [Lombok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lombok_Strait), carry international traffic between the [Indian](14580.md) and [Pacific](23070.md) oceans, including ships transporting energy supplies and commercial cargo.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOegroseno201549-135][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott2019196,_199-136]&#x20;

The number of Indonesian islands varies by counting method, with published figures ranging from 13,466 named islands to more than 17,500.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESukendra2017204,_207-137] About 922 of them are permanently inhabited.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndréfouëtPaulFarhan20222-138] Its five main islands are [Sumatra](28679.md), [Java](69336.md), [Sulawesi](28740.md), [Borneo](4518.md), and [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea). Indonesia occupies most of Borneo, where it is known as [Kalimantan](165470.md), and the western part of New Guinea, commonly called [Papua](1432822.md). The remainder of those islands is shared with [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei) and [Malaysia](3607937.md), and with [Papua New Guinea](22943.md), respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrederickWorden201199-139]&#x20;

Java accounts for less than 7% of Indonesia's land area but is its most populous island, with intensive farming and extensive urban growth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_KoninckHai2025376,_378,_379–380-140] Forest cover also differs widely, with larger forested areas in Papua and Maluku than in Java and Bali.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESantoroPirasYu20253129,_3131-141] Indonesia's terrain includes high peaks, volcanic lakes, major rivers, and coastal plains. At 4,884 metres (16,024 ft), [Puncak Jaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puncak_Jaya) on New Guinea is Indonesia's highest peak,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Lorentz_National_Park"._UNESCO-142] while [Lake Toba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Toba) on Sumatra is its largest lake.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Toba_Caldera_UNESCO_Global_Geoparks"._UNESCO-143] In [Kalimantan](165470.md), major waterways like the [Kapuas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapuas_River), [Barito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barito_River), and [Mahakam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahakam_River) rivers have historically served as corridors for movement and trade between communities in the island's interior and those near the coast.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELubisSusantoHarjoko202112-144]&#x20;

Indonesia occupies a complex plate-boundary zone where the [Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_plate), [India-Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Australian_plate), and [Pacific-Philippine sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Sea_plate) [tectonic plates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonics) interact, with subduction zones along these boundaries producing frequent earthquakes and volcanic activity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2009454-145][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECummins20173–4-146] The resulting volcanic belt contains roughly 400 volcanoes, around 130 of which are considered active.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHidayat20201-147] This tectonic setting also exposes the archipelago to major earthquake and tsunami hazards. The [Sumatra earthquake in 2004 generated a tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_Ocean_earthquake_and_tsunami) that caused widespread destruction in [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPutra201259,_62-148] The archipelago has also experienced very large eruptions. No bigger explosive event is known from the past 2.5 million years than the [Toba super-eruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youngest_Toba_eruption) about 74,000 years ago,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeGao202024-149] and the [eruption of Tambora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1815_eruption_of_Mount_Tambora) in 1815 was followed by global cooling and the [Year Without a Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Year_Without_a_Summer) in 1816.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGertisserSelf2015135-150]&#x20;

### Climate

Main articles: [Climate of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Indonesia) and [Climate change in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Indonesia)

Satellite view of the Indonesian archipelago largely covered by dense clouds.

Rain clouds over Indonesia during the monsoon season, observed by the [Himawari-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himawari_8) weather satellite in March 2020.

Indonesia's equatorial position and [monsoon winds](57630.md) give it a tropical climate. Most lowland areas are warm and humid throughout the year, while higher terrain is cooler and seasonal temperature differences are small.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Climate_Risk_Profile:_Indonesia_(2021)"._World_Bank_Group_and_Asian_Development_Bank,_20216-151] Seasonal changes in the monsoon generally bring wetter conditions from November to April and drier conditions from May to October, but their timing and intensity vary by region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Climate_Risk_Profile:_Indonesia_(2021)"._World_Bank_Group_and_Asian_Development_Bank,_20216-151][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAs-syakur20137724-152] Much of the country has a [tropical rainforest climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest_climate), with tropical monsoon and savanna climates across parts of the islands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParamitaMatzarakis2023286-153] Seasonal rainfall helps set rice-planting calendars and influences droughts, floods, and fires.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaylor20077753-154][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaruniasaPambudi2022181-155][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpessa2015429–431-156]&#x20;

Rainfall varies across the archipelago. Western Sumatra, western Java, and portions of Kalimantan and Papua receive the highest annual rainfall, whereas regions in closer proximity to Australia—such as [Nusa Tenggara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Sunda_Islands)—experience a significantly drier climate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZainiVonnisaMarzukiRamadhan20239901-157] The surrounding oceans, island geography, monsoon winds, and topography all affect where and when rain falls.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAs-syakur20137723-158] In drought-prone regions of the country, [El Niño](317311.md) can suppress rainfall and prolong dry periods, causing water shortages and crop losses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaruniasaPambudi2022180–181,_183–184-159][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaylor20077752–7753-160]&#x20;

Indonesia already faces climate-related hazards. Climate projections indicate further warming and sea-level rise, changes in rainfall patterns, and greater risks from extreme heat and flooding.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Climate_Risk_Profile:_Indonesia_(2021)"._World_Bank_Group_and_Asian_Development_Bank,_20212-161][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMercoglianoRederEllena202115-162] These changes affect agriculture, water resources, public health, coastal settlements, and wildfire management.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Climate_Risk_Profile:_Indonesia_(2021)"._World_Bank_Group_and_Asian_Development_Bank,_20212,_15,_21,_23,_27-163][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECaseArdiansyahSpector20072,_9–10-164] [Sea-level rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level_rise) is especially relevant to coastal areas, where much of Indonesia's population and infrastructure is located.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeumann20151,_14-165][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Climate_Risk_Profile:_Indonesia_(2021)"._World_Bank_Group_and_Asian_Development_Bank,_202116–17-166] Evidence from [Pekalongan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekalongan) in Central Java suggests that households with fewer economic resources and communities with weaker infrastructure may have less capacity to adapt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkhmadiRahmithaWahyu201210–11,_31-167]&#x20;

### Biodiversity and conservation

Main articles: [Fauna of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Indonesia), [Flora of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_of_Indonesia), [Environment of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_Indonesia), and [Conservation in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_in_Indonesia)

See also: [Protected areas of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Indonesia), [List of national parks of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Indonesia), and [List of biosphere reserves of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_biosphere_reserves_of_Indonesia)

A Komodo dragon on rocky ground with its forked tongue extended.

The [Komodo dragon](17360.md) (*Varanus komodoensis*) is one of Indonesia's officially designated national animals under a presidential decree in 1993.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Presidential_Decree_Number_4_of_1993_concerning_National_Animals_and_Flowers",_1993-168]

Indonesia is among the 17 countries identified as [megadiverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megadiverse_countries) by [Conservation International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_International). Its many islands and geological past have helped produce a diverse flora and fauna.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_-_Country_Profile"._Convention_on_Biological_Diversity-169][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvon_Rintelen20171-170] Within the archipelago, [Wallacea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallacea) is a transition zone between Asian and Australian fauna and one of the world's largest centres of endemism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStruebig20221118-171] In 2015, an estimated 83% of Southeast Asia's old-growth forest was in Indonesia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEstoqueOobaAvitabileHijioka20195-172] Mangrove forests, coral reefs and seagrass beds are among Indonesia's coastal ecosystems.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutomoMoosa200588-173]&#x20;

Large [oil-palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_oil_production_in_Indonesia) and timber plantations were estimated to account for more than two-fifths of [national deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Indonesia) between 2001 and 2016.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustinSchwantesGuKasibhatla20191-174] Conversion of forest land for farming, roads, and other development breaks up remaining habitat, which affects species populations, habitat quality, and biodiversity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGunawan20241–2,_5–6,_10-175] Peat-swamp forests are important carbon stores and habitat for threatened [taxa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxon), but are vulnerable to logging, fire and land conversion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPosa201149-176] In the ocean, coral diversity has been found lower on polluted or anchor-damaged reefs, and coral cover has declined sharply under extreme, prolonged ocean heat.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdinger1998617-177][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERazak202610–11-178]&#x20;

Indonesia has designated 554 protected areas covering 27 million hectares, equivalent to 14% of its land area.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENugraha2024474-179] Assessments conducted in 2015, 2017 and 2019 found improved management scores, but identified poaching, illegal logging, human settlements, tourism and non-timber cultivation as the main threats to protected areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENugraha2024474-179] In Papua, research around the [Cyclops Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclops_Mountains) protected area found limited consultation with nearby communities and implementation problems linked to scarce resources.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnes2023360-180]&#x20;

## Government and politics

Main article: [Politics of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Indonesia)

See also: [Government of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Indonesia)

Rows of delegates fill the large MPR parliamentary chamber facing a central stage.

A presidential inauguration by the MPR in the [Parliament Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MPR/DPR/DPD_building) Jakarta, [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Joko_Widodo)

Indonesia is a unitary republic with a presidential system under the [1945 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Indonesia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20192,_3-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamzahNarangYusari20212-182] The Constitution's five principles of *[Pancasila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pancasila)* are taught through civic education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20191-183][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDewantara2019400–401-184] After the [fall of the New Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Suharto) in 1998, four [constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_Indonesia) between 1999 and 2002 altered the political system by introducing direct presidential elections, establishing new state institutions, reducing the MPR's role, and giving regional autonomy a constitutional basis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarijantiLindsey2006138–139-185] Indonesia remained a unitary state, while decentralisation transferred substantial powers to the provinces, districts, and cities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButt20102,_5–7-186]&#x20;

The [president](10011631.md) is [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state), [head of government](37570.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamzahNarangYusari20212-182] and commander-in-chief of the [Indonesian National Armed Forces](14650.md), and may serve no more than two five-year terms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20193,_4,_7-187] The [People's Consultative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Consultative_Assembly) (MPR) consists of elected members of the [House of Representatives](737841.md) (DPR) and the [Regional Representative Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Representative_Council) (DPD).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20192-188] The MPR may amend the constitution, inaugurates the president and vice president, and may dismiss them under constitutional procedures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20192,_4–6-189] The DPR makes laws with the president, approves the state budget, and oversees the government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_201910–11,_14-190] The DPD may propose, review, and advise on bills concerning regional government,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamzahNarangYusari20212-182] but has less authority than the DPR.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESinukaban202017-191]&#x20;

Indonesia's legal system belongs primarily to the [civil-law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) tradition, while also incorporating customary (*[adat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adat)*) law and Islamic law, including in marriage and inheritance matters for Muslims.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReniRenaldi2019-192] [Judicial power](19410106.md) is exercised by the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Indonesia), the courts beneath it, and the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Indonesia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButtBisariyadiSiregar2026107-193] The Supreme Court acts as the final appellate court and reviews regulations below statutes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReniRenaldi2019-192] The Constitutional Court reviews statutes for constitutionality and decides disputes over state-institution powers, political party dissolution, and election results.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_201916-194] It also has a role in presidential impeachment proceedings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20194,_17-195] The Religious Courts hear marriage, divorce and inheritance cases, as well as certain economic disputes arising under Islamic law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECammackFeener201227–30-196] The [Judicial Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_Commission_of_Indonesia) proposes candidates for Supreme Court justices and has responsibilities concerning judicial conduct.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_201916-194]&#x20;

### Parties and elections

Main articles: [List of political parties in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Indonesia) and [Elections in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Indonesia)

Prabowo Subianto in a black suit, red tie and black peci beside the Indonesian flag.

[Prabowo Subianto](2829084.md)\
[President](10011631.md)

Gibran Rakabuming Raka in a black suit, red tie and black peci beside the Indonesian flag.

[Gibran Rakabuming Raka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibran_Rakabuming_Raka)\
[Vice President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_Indonesia)

Since the return to [competitive elections in 1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Indonesian_legislative_election), Indonesia has had a fragmented [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPepinsky2025346–348-197] No party has won a majority in [legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Indonesia),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPepinsky2025348-198] and presidents have usually governed through broad coalitions that include many parties in the legislature and cabinet.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPepinsky2025346,_348-199][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlater201824,_27-200]&#x20;

Political parties are often distinguished by their orientation toward secular nationalism or political Islam,[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFossati2019143-202] but governing coalitions commonly include both.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlater201829-203] Party politics often relies more on patronage and elite power-sharing than detailed policy programmes,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFionnaTomsa20172–3,_21–22-204] although the role of Islam in public life is a source of ideological difference.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFossati20204,_10-205] Opposition parties have at times joined presidential power-sharing arrangements after elections.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlater201836,_40–41-206]&#x20;

Indonesia held its first [general election in 1955](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Indonesian_legislative_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAziz1955400-207] Since [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indonesian_presidential_election), voters have directly elected the president, the DPR, and the DPD every five years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Examining_the_2024_Political_Competition"._Indonesian_General_Elections_Commission,_2021-208] Members of the DPR are elected through party-based contests,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_201913-209] while DPD candidates run as individuals to represent provincial constituencies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESinukaban202019-210] National elections require large-scale logistics, as ballots and other materials must reach remote islands, mountain areas, and places with limited infrastructure.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEErviana20241376-211]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Subdivisions of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_Indonesia)

Indonesia is a unitary state divided into [provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Indonesia), [regencies, and cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regencies_and_cities_in_Indonesia), each with elected regional authorities regulated by law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20199-212] Provinces are headed by governors and have provincial legislatures (DPRD). Regencies and cities are headed by regents and mayors and have local legislatures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_20199-212] Below regencies and cities are [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Indonesia), which are divided into [villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villages_of_Indonesia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESyukri202149-213] Rural villages have self-governing status, while urban villages form part of district administration.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESyukri2021161-214]&#x20;

Regional autonomy after 1998 transferred substantial powers to subnational governments, including the provinces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButt20106-215] Special arrangements apply to nine of them: [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh) may apply aspects of Islamic law,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller2004333–334-216] the six provinces in [Papua](1432822.md#Current_government) have special autonomy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmilianusLumbuunLatifSinaga202410–11-217] and [Yogyakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Region_of_Yogyakarta) retains a hereditary sultanate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWiszowatyWahyuni2023322-218] [Jakarta](16275.md) has autonomy due to its role as the national capital.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOkamoto201413-219] Since 2022, [Nusantara](69803286.md) in [East Kalimantan](453651.md) has been planned as the future national capital.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Law_Number_3_of_2022_concerning_the_National_Capital",_2022-220]&#x20;
