### Foreign relations and military

Main articles: [Foreign relations of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Indonesia) and [Indonesian National Armed Forces](14650.md)

The tiered white ASEAN headquarters building behind a landscaped garden in Jakarta.

Jakarta hosts the [headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASEAN_Headquarters) of [ASEAN](106539.md).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Agreement_on_the_Establishment_of_the_ASEAN_Secretariat"._ASEAN,_1976-221]

Indonesia follows an "independent and active" (*bebas aktif*) foreign policy, a doctrine formulated by Vice President [Mohammad Hatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Hatta) in 1948.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia's_Foreign_Policy"._Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia-222] The policy has emphasised non-alignment and independent action, along with a preference for diplomacy over military means in advancing regional objectives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnwar201858–59-223][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShekhar2022133,_139-224] As a middle power, Indonesia works through regional and multilateral institutions while preserving room to manoeuvre in its relations with major powers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUmar20231459–1460,_1464–1465-225]&#x20;

Indonesian Military Academy cadets in blue dress uniforms and white long pants standing in formation.

[Indonesian Military Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Military_Academy) cadets

As a founding member of the [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md) (ASEAN), Indonesia places the organisation at the centre of its regional diplomacy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUmar20231460,_1470-226] including in managing relations with major powers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnwar2020123-227] Indonesia has broad bilateral ties with [Malaysia](31449093.md) and [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia%E2%80%93Singapore_relations), including cooperation in economic, defence, and cultural fields.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"State_Visit_by_the_President_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia_to_Malaysia_27_January_2025"._Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_Malaysia,_2025-228][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs,_Singapore-229] [Relations with Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Indonesia_relations) are conducted through a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership covering several areas, including economic development and regional stability.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Plan_of_Action_for_the_Indonesia-Australia_Comprehensive_Strategic_Partnership_(2025–2029)"._Australian_Government_Department_of_Foreign_Affairs_and_Trade.-230] On [China](5405.md)–[United States](3434750.md) competition, Indonesia has avoided alliances and taking sides, emphasised an ASEAN-led regional approach, and sought to limit escalation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShekhar2022136-231]&#x20;

Indonesia has been a member of the [United Nations](31769.md) since 1950,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Member_States_-_United_Nations"._United_Nations-232] apart from an [absence in 1965–66](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_from_the_United_Nations#Indonesia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_-_United_Nations"._United_Nations-233][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"What_happened_when_Indonesia_'withdrew'_from_the_United_Nations"._Rappler,_2016-234] It participates in multilateral forums including the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement), the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md), and the [East Asia Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia_Summit).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Independent_UNIDO_Country_Evaluation:_The_Republic_of_Indonesia"._United_Nations_Industrial_Development_Organization,_20158-235] After decades as a major recipient of foreign aid,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChowdhurySugema2005186–187-236] Indonesia has been providing development assistance since 2015 and established a foreign aid agency in 2019.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAzisSatriawangsa202451,_54-237] Since 1957, it has sent military and police personnel to [UN peacekeeping](15652027.md) missions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"United_Nations_thanks_Indonesia_for_its_contribution_to_Peacekeeping"._United_Nations,_2024-238]&#x20;

The [Indonesian National Armed Forces](14650.md) (TNI) consists of the [Army](1711324.md), [Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Navy), and [Air Force](8842734.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Duties_of_the_Indonesian_Army,_Navy,_and_Air_Force_Pursuant_to_Law_No._34_of_2004"._Indonesian_National_Armed_Forces-239] The Army's territorial units extend across provincial and regency/city areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYabuki2024173-240] Until its termination in 2000, the military's "dual function" (*[dwifungsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwifungsi)*) doctrine provided a basis for involvement in political affairs alongside its security role.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMietzner200612,_37-241] By 2004, reforms had ended unelected military representation in Parliament and removed the military faction from the MPR.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMietzner200661-242] A 2024 study found that cooperative parliamentary oversight had helped stabilise civil–military relations but also slowed further reform and weakened democratic management of defence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENgKurniawan2024698–699-243]&#x20;

### Law enforcement and human rights

Main articles: [Indonesian National Police](18251498.md) and [Human rights in Indonesia](4409435.md)

A man carries an office chair past burning furniture and a crowd during the May 1998 riots in Jakarta.

The [May 1998 riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_1998_Indonesia_riots) in Jakarta involved attacks on people and property that targeted [Chinese Indonesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Indonesians).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHimawan2022536,_538-244]

Law enforcement in Indonesia is primarily carried out by the [Indonesian National Police](18251498.md) (POLRI). The [Chief of the Indonesian National Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Indonesian_National_Police) is directly responsible to the President.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Unfinished_Business:_Police_Accountability_in_Indonesia"._Amnesty_International,_200917,_59-245] POLRI's duties include maintaining public order and security, protecting and serving the public, and enforcing the law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Regulation_of_the_Indonesian_National_Police_Number_3_of_2024_concerning_Amendments_to_Regulation_of_the_Indonesian_National_Police_Number_14_of_2018_concerning_the_Organisational_Structure_and_Working_Procedures_of_Regional_Police_Forces",_2024-246]&#x20;

The [National Commission on Human Rights](36463750.md) (Komnas HAM) is Indonesia's national human-rights institution and is authorised to investigate human rights violations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_Nations_Human_Rights_Committee20242-247] Accountability for past abuses has been limited. In 2024, the [United Nations Human Rights Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Committee) noted that only 4 of 16 violations investigated by Komnas HAM had reached the courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_Nations_Human_Rights_Committee20243–4-248] Indonesia practices the [death penalty](36861341.md) but has not executed anyone since 2016.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Global_executions_hit_record_high_as_Indonesia_continues_to_hand_down_death_sentences"._Amnesty_International_Indonesia,_2026-249]&#x20;

[Anti-Chinese racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination_against_Chinese_Indonesians) has persisted despite the removal of most discriminatory policies after the New Order.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETanasaldy2022460-250] Since 2016, [anti-LGBTQ campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Indonesia) have included hostile statements by public officials, police raids and proposals for criminal restrictions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERodríguezMurtagh20223–6-251] Religious minorities have also been affected by the country's [blasphemy law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemy_law_in_Indonesia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_Nations_Human_Rights_Committee202410-252] In Papua, human rights monitors have reported extrajudicial killings and disappearances, restrictions on peaceful expression, and displacement due to security operations and [armed conflict](21657843.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaird202525,_43-253]&#x20;

## Economy

Main articles: [Economy of Indonesia](14647.md), [Agriculture in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Indonesia), [Poverty in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Indonesia), and [Tourism in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Indonesia)

Aerial view of modern skyscrapers of Jakarta and a wide-city boulevard surrounding a large circular fountain roundabout amid a dense urban landscape under a clear blue sky.

[Jakarta](16275.md) is Indonesia's principal financial centre.

Rows of oil palms in a plantation in Riau.

[Palm oil](57561.md) plantation in [Kampar Regency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kampar_Regency), [Riau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riau). Indonesia is the world's largest producer of palm oil.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPachecoGnychDermawanKomarudin20177-254]

The terraced stone structure and bell-shaped stupas of Borobudur rising above surrounding greenery.

[Borobudur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borobudur) in [Central Java](453584.md), Indonesia's most visited tourist attraction

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELauderLauder20176-255]

Indonesia has a [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) in which the [private sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector) and the government both have large roles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein202450-256][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank20243-257] It is the only [G20](2206608.md) member state in Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"G20_Members"._G20-258] In 2025, Indonesia's gross domestic product was Rp23.821 quadrillion (US\$1.45 trillion) at current prices,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia's_Economic_Growth_in_2025_was_5.11_Percent"._Statistics_Indonesia,_2026-259][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_-_Data"._World_Bank,_2025-260] making it Southeast Asia's largest economy and placing it among the world's [top 20 by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and [top 10 by GDP at purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBreuerKinda20183-261] Services and industry account for the largest shares of gross domestic product,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein20247-262] while agriculture remains a major source of employment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Labor_Market_Indicators_Indonesia_August_2025"._Statistics_Indonesia,_2025vii–viii-263]&#x20;

The economy was once largely agricultural, and early post-independence policy centred on agricultural self-sufficiency. Shift towards industrial and urban growth gathered pace from the late 1960s, and sped up in the 1980s as lower oil prices pushed the economy toward manufactured exports.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEliasNoone201133-264] Export-oriented manufacturing expanded from the mid-1980s to the mid-1990s, during a period of strong economic growth and declining poverty.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAswicahyonoHill201794-265][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENugrohoAmirMaududyMarlina20211205-266] The [Asian financial crisis of 1997–98](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis) caused a severe contraction,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETitiheruwAtje20089–10-267] but growth resumed in 1999 and was followed by reforms to banking, fiscal policy and the exchange-rate regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasri201838-268]&#x20;

Consumer spending makes up a large share of GDP, along with population growth, urbanisation and an expanding consuming class.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERazdan20134-269] Domestic demand helped soften the effect of the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), while exports, public consumption and private consumption contributed to recovery after the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasriRahardja2011170-270][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank20221-271] Informal employment is widespread, particularly in agriculture and low-skilled services. Informal firms tend to have lower productivity and pay, and workers outside the bigger firms have more limited access to social insurance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein202445-272] Businesses also have to contend with corruption, burdensome and sometimes conflicting local regulations, in addition to unreliable infrastructure such as power outages and congested roads.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoccero20086–9,_14-273] The official poverty rate was about 8% in March 2026.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"In_March_2026,_the_percentage_of_the_poor_population_decreased_to_8.07_percent"._Statistics_Indonesia,_2026-274]&#x20;

The country's many islands affect where economic activity takes place and how goods move across the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERothenbergTemenggung20191-275] Economic activity is concentrated on Java,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCullochSjahrir200812-276] with Jakarta in particular accounting for a disproportionate share of national output.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"\[2010_Version\]_Distribution_of_GRDP_to_Total_GRDP_of_38_Provinces_at_Current_Market_Prices_by_Province_(Percent),_2025"._Statistics_Indonesia,_2026-277] Meanwhile, much of eastern Indonesia has weaker infrastructure and economies more dependent on agriculture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCullochSjahrir200812-276] High domestic transport costs hinder production and investment outside Java and make it harder to connect producers in the outer islands with major markets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERothenbergTemenggung20191-275][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank201512-278]&#x20;

Tourism is a major service industry, generating about [US\$](18717338.md)14 billion in foreign-exchange earnings and recording 11.6 million international visitor arrivals in 2023.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDávidRahmatPriatmoko20242-279] Tourism-related jobs and enterprises include accommodation, food services, passenger transport, travel agencies and other activities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOECD2022321-280] International tourism is concentrated in [Bali](4147.md) and other major gateways,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOllivaudHaxton20198-281] while domestic travellers account for most tourism spending.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOECD2022321-280]&#x20;

Indonesia spends little on research and development, with limited private-sector involvement and weak coordination in government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDamuriAswicahyonoChristian201899,_102,_106-282] The government established the aircraft manufacturer later renamed [Indonesian Aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Aerospace) in 1976 as part of an industrial-transformation strategy, beginning with licensed production before moving into joint aircraft development.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESteenhuisde_Bruijn20045–6-283] The [Palapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palapa) domestic satellite system extended communications to remote parts of Indonesia through a network of earth stations and was also used to distribute television and radio programmes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEITU1994715-284]&#x20;

Natural-resource industries affect both investment and economic growth, which can rise or fall with commodity prices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank202024-285] Policies since 2020 have encouraged more processing of commodities at home, particularly [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein202456–57-286] Indonesia's exports include coal and petroleum gas, as well as palm oil, coffee and spices. Its main trading partners are mostly in Asia, with the United States also among the largest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia"._The_Observatory_of_Economic_Complexity,_2024-287] Indonesia takes part in regional economic cooperation through ASEAN and [APEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC) and is integrated into the wider global economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuryanta202111-288]&#x20;

## Infrastructure

### Transport and connectivity

Main article: [Transport in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Indonesia)

The cargo ship Logistik Nusantara 3, with a dark hull and two orange deck cranes, on calm water with mountains in the background.

Sea transport is crucial for transporting essential goods across Indonesia's many islands.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESofiyandiKurniawanYudhistira20231027–1029-289]

Road transport is the predominant mode in Indonesia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_transitions_to_EVs,_builds_ports_and_expands_airports"._Oxford_Business_Group,_2024-290] Reliance on cars and [motorcycles](19876.md) is high because public transport in many cities has not kept pace with urban growth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIngham20199-291] Rail transport is concentrated on Java, but new lines have recently begun operating in South Sulawesi. The [Jakarta–Bandung high-speed railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail_in_Indonesia), the first of its kind in the region, began commercial operations in 2023.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_transitions_to_EVs,_builds_ports_and_expands_airports"._Oxford_Business_Group,_2024-290]&#x20;

Maritime and air networks are important for linking population centres across the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_transitions_to_EVs,_builds_ports_and_expands_airports"._Oxford_Business_Group,_2024-290] Connectivity is weaker in eastern Indonesia, where higher logistics costs contribute to regional price differences.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZenYudhistira2022viii-292] In the telecommunications sector, the Palapa Ring project uses terrestrial and submarine fibre as a core national internet network, but there are service gaps in some remote regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein202472-293]&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Indonesia](31676698.md)

Large piles of black coal beneath an elevated conveyor system under a blue sky.

[Coal mining](208413.md) at the [East Kutai coal mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Kutai_coal_mine), [East Kalimantan](453651.md). Coal reserves in Indonesia is among the world's largest.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEIA20259-294]

In 2023, Indonesia produced nearly twice as much primary energy as it consumed, with [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal) dominating both primary energy production and electricity generation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEIA20252-295] The country exports large volumes of coal and [LNG](832128.md) and imports substantial amounts of refined petroleum products.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEIA202518–20-296] The state-owned electricity company, [Perusahaan Listrik Negara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perusahaan_Listrik_Negara) (PLN), controls the country's transmission and distribution networks and purchases electricity from independent power producers for supply to the national grids.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIEA202019-297]&#x20;

Renewables supplied a small amount of Indonesia's electricity in 2023, although the country ranked first globally in geothermal generation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEIA202511–12-298] Investment in renewable energy has been hindered by regulatory uncertainty and PLN's financial capacity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEIA20258,_10-299]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Indonesia) and [Indonesians](4885606.md)

See also: [List of Indonesian cities by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indonesian_cities_by_population) and [List of metropolitan areas in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Indonesia)

Indonesia's population density is shown highest across much of Java and around major cities, and lower across most outer-island regions.

A map of districts (*kecamatan*) coloured by population density, measured in people per square kilometre

Indonesia's [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Indonesian_census) recorded 270.2 million people,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Results_of_the_2020_Population_Census"._Statistics_Indonesia,_20219-7] making it the world's [fourth most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) after India, China and the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_Nations_Department_of_Economic_and_Social_Affairs,_Population_Division202436-300] The population is concentrated on Java and in urban centres, while many provinces outside Java and Bali are much less densely populated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETejasmara2023117–118-301] Java is home to 56.1% of the population,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Results_of_the_2020_Population_Census"._Statistics_Indonesia,_202111-302] and its population density is far above the national average,[^cite_note-303] reaching 1,171 people per square kilometre (3,030 people/mi<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMardiansjah20232-304]&#x20;

Indonesia has a median age of 30.7 years as of 2026,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Median_age_of_population_–_Indonesia"._United_Nations_Department_of_Economic_and_Social_Affairs,_Population_Division,_2026-305] while about 69% of the population was aged 15–64 in 2025.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Results_of_the_2025_Intercensal_Population_Survey_(SUPAS_2025)"._Statistics_Indonesia,_20264-306] [Jakarta](16275.md) is the country's largest metropolitan area and, under the UN's harmonised urban definition, is listed as the world's most populous city, with nearly 42 million inhabitants as of 2025.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"World_Urbanization_Prospects_2025:_Summary_of_Results"._United_Nations_Department_of_Economic_and_Social_Affairs,_Population_Division,_2025107-310]&#x20;

Approximately 59% of Indonesians lived in urban areas as of 2025.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Share_of_people_living_in_urban_areas,_2025"._Our_World_in_Data,_2025-311] Urbanisation has come from rural-to-urban migration, natural increase in urban areas, and the reclassification of rural settlements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETejasmara2023132-312] It has also expanded metropolitan regions, especially on Java,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMardiansjah202111,_17-313] and increased demand for infrastructure, basic services, and city management.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETejasmara2023135-314] In addition, about 8 million [Indonesians reside overseas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_diaspora), with large communities in [Malaysia](3607937.md), the [Netherlands](21148.md), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), [Singapore](27318.md), and [Taiwan](25734.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESetijadi20171,_7-315]&#x20;

### Ethnic groups and languages

Main articles: [Ethnic groups in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Indonesia), [Native Indonesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Indonesians), and [Languages of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Indonesia)

Ethnic groups are shown distributed across distinct regions of the Indonesian archipelago.

A map of ethnic groups in Indonesia

The [2010 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Indonesian_census) listed over 1,300 *suku bangsa*, or ethnic groups.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Citizenship,_Ethnicity,_Religion,_and_Everyday_Language_of_the_Indonesian_Population"._Statistics_Indonesia,_20105-316] A later study in 2015 reclassified them into about 600 groups after noting classification difficulties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnanta2015xv,_28,_39-317] [Austronesian-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages) populations predominate in much of western and central Indonesia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor20035–7-318] Melanesian populations live mainly in the eastern islands, including parts of Nusa Tenggara, Maluku, and Papua.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor20035–7-318][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee20066-319] The [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_people) are the largest ethnic group, making up about 40% of the population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnanta20153-320] Other major groups include the [Sundanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundanese_people), [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Indonesians), [Batak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batak), [Madurese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madurese_people), [Betawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betawi_people), [Minangkabau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minangkabau_people), and [Bugis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bugis_people).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnanta201578-321][^cite_note-325]&#x20;

The official language, [Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language), is a standardized variety of [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language) based on the Riau-Johor literary tradition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESneddon20038,_69–70-326] Malay had long served as a *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)* in the archipelago before Indonesian nationalists adopted it as a language of unity through the 1928 [Youth Pledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_Pledge).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESneddon20035-327] It gained official and formal status in 1945 under the name *Bahasa Indonesia*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESneddon20039-328] Written in the [Latin script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_script),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Chapter_17:_Southeast_Asia-II"._The_Unicode_Consortium-329] Indonesian has spread through education, government, and mass media, and serves as a common language across ethnic and regional boundaries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESteinhauer1994773-330]&#x20;

More than 700 languages are spoken across the archipelago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAji20227226-331] They are divided into two major language groups, [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages) and [Papuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papuan_languages).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee20067-332] [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_language) is the most widely spoken local language and has official regional status in [Yogyakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Region_of_Yogyakarta).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAji20227227-333][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Regional_Regulation_of_the_Province_of_the_Special_Region_of_Yogyakarta_Number_2_of_2021_concerning_the_Preservation_and_Development_of_the_Javanese_Language,_Literature,_and_Script",_2021-334] Several regional languages in Indonesia have traditional writing systems of their own, some of which survive in only limited use today.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJukes20141-335][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustin20141,_4-336] Local languages remain part of regional identity and cultural heritage, even as Indonesian dominates national public life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESneddon20036–7,_208-337] [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) never became widely spoken as Malay was already the archipelago's lingua franca.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESalverda2013805-338] However, it remains important in some legal contexts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell201449-339]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Indonesia)

The white Baiturrahman Grand Mosque with black domes reflected in a foreground pool.

[Baiturrahman Grand Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baiturrahman_Grand_Mosque) in [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh). Northern Sumatra saw one of the earliest Islamic sultanates in Southeast Asia.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeener20191-340]

Rows of white-clad Hindu worshippers gathered before the tiered shrines of Besakih Temple.

A Hindu prayer ceremony at [Besakih Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Besakih_Temple) in [Bali](4147.md), Indonesia's only [Hindu-majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_Hinduism) province.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDaniel20173–4-341]

Indonesia recognises [six religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Indonesia): [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Indonesia), [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Indonesia), [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism_in_Indonesia), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Indonesia), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Indonesia), and [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_for_the_Confucian_Religion_in_Indonesia),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshall201886-342] while the constitution guarantees freedom of religion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_201921-343] The state regulates religious affiliation and public worship. Recognised religions receive administrative recognition for religious affiliation, marriages, and houses of worship, but followers of [indigenous religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aliran_Kepercayaan) often receive weaker recognition or face discrimination.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshall201886–88-344] *Pancasila* makes belief in one God one of the state ideology's five principles and is used to support national unity and religious harmony.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGłąb20205-345] Religious minorities still face discrimination, including through local religious bylaws, social pressure, and restrictions on worship.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshall201888,_90-346][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESumaktoyo202023–25-347]&#x20;

Indonesia has [the largest Muslim population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_by_country) in the world,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"5_facts_about_Muslims_and_Christians_in_Indonesia"._Pew_Research_Center,_2024-348] with 251 million Muslims in 2025, or 87.15% of the population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Religious_Diversity_in_Indonesia_According_to_Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_Population_Data"._Tempo,_2026-4][^cite_note-351] Christians made up 10.44% of the population and formed majorities in several eastern provinces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Religious_Diversity_in_Indonesia_According_to_Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_Population_Data"._Tempo,_2026-4][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAritonangSteenbrink2008419-352] Hindus accounted for 1.66% and are concentrated in Bali,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Religious_Diversity_in_Indonesia_According_to_Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_Population_Data"._Tempo,_2026-4][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDaniel20173–4-341] while Buddhists accounted for 0.69% and are found mainly among [Chinese Indonesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Indonesians), though not exclusively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Religious_Diversity_in_Indonesia_According_to_Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_Population_Data"._Tempo,_2026-4][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Negotiating_the_Cultural_and_the_Religious:_The_Recasting_of_the_Chinese_Indonesian_Buddhist"._BiblioAsia,_2011-353]&#x20;

Many local belief systems predate major world religions and continue alongside them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessing200613–15-354][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatts20154-355] They include beliefs in ancestors, spirits, and powers associated with nature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWessing200611,_16-356][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatts20153-357] Traditions such as [Sunda Wiwitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunda_Wiwitan), [Kejawèn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kejaw%C3%A8n), and [Kaharingan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaharingan) continue in different forms within or alongside the recognised religions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMutaqin20142–3-358][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeertz19765-359][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaier2007568-360] In Java and Bali, Islam and Hinduism also absorbed older customs and ritual practices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicardMadinier2011142–143-361][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAli201112,_15-362]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Indonesia)

The University of Indonesia rectorate, a tall tiered building with a steep pyramidal roof, framed by trees and lower red-roofed buildings.

[University of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Indonesia) rectorate campus in Depok

Indonesia's education system includes more than 50 million students and over 250,000 schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"World_Bank_and_Education_in_Indonesia"._World_Bank,_2024-363] It is managed across ministries responsible for school education, higher education, and religious education,[^cite_note-364] and follows a 6-3-3-4 structure: six years of elementary school, three years each of junior and senior secondary school, and four years of tertiary education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMukmininHabibiPrasojoIdi201956-365]&#x20;

Since independence, schools have supported national integration through Indonesian-language instruction, national curricula, and civic education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrake198968,_74-366] Access to education and school quality vary, particularly between urban and rural areas and among regions with differing levels of development.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuttaqin201819–20-367][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAditiaSzell20251,_14-368] Teacher quality and distribution are also persistent issues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERosserFahmi201872-369] Student learning outcomes are also low by OECD benchmarks. In [PISA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) 2022, Indonesian 15-year-olds performed below OECD averages in mathematics, reading and science, with most not reaching the minimum proficiency level in each subject.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOECD20232–3-370]&#x20;

Government spending on education accounted for approximately 1.3% of GDP in 2023.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Government_expenditure_on_education,_total_(%_of_GDP)_-_Indonesia"._World_Bank,_2023-371] In 2022, Indonesia had 4,481 higher education institutions, including universities, Islamic institutions, service colleges, and open universities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoeliodihardjo2024114-372] The [University of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Indonesia), [Gadjah Mada University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadjah_Mada_University), and the [Bandung Institute of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandung_Institute_of_Technology) are among the country's highest-ranked public universities in international rankings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESukoco2021566-373] Higher education has expanded since 1990 and contributes to better employment, but access, participation, and institutional quality are uneven.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEADB20182-374][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOECD202180–81,_85-375]&#x20;

### Healthcare

Main article: [Healthcare in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Indonesia)

A white multi-story building with illuminated windows

Pondok Indah Hospital in [South Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jakarta)

Primary care is provided mainly through community health centres (*[puskesmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puskesmas)*) and primary-care hospitals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWenang20212-376] In 2014, Indonesia launched *[Jaminan Kesehatan Nasional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Indonesia#Universal_health_coverage)* (JKN), one of the world's largest [single-payer health-insurance systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_healthcare) that covered 98% of the population by 2024. Despite the near-universal coverage, where people live still affects the availability of care and healthcare personnel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESusilo20251-377]&#x20;

Life expectancy increased between 1990 and 2019 from 62.5 to 69.4 years for men and from 65.7 to 73.5 years for women, but there are substantial differences between provinces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMboiSyailendrawatiOstroffElyazar2022e1632-378] Indonesia faces a double burden of disease. Non-communicable conditions have become increasingly prominent in overall health loss, and infectious diseases continue to contribute substantially to disability and early mortality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMboiSyailendrawatiOstroffElyazar2022e1632,_e1636-379] Stunting prevalence among children was 21.6% in 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELestari202321-380]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Indonesia)

See also: [National Intangible Cultural Heritage of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_of_Indonesia) and [Public holidays in Indonesia](1200613.md)

Indonesia's cultures differ across islands, languages, and communities. Older populations and later Austronesian migrations contributed to this diversity, with Papuan and Melanesian populations prominent in the east.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee20065–6-381][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor20037-382] Movement by sea placed the Indonesian archipelago within trading and migration networks involving Indian, Arab, Persian, Chinese, and mainland Southeast Asian peoples, followed later by Europeans. Religious and courtly influences entered through these routes, and European expansion ultimately brought colonial domination. Coastal commercial centres often encountered outside influences before they reached inland communities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee20065,_8–9-383][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVickers20132-384]&#x20;

Indonesia has no single uniform cultural tradition. Regional traditions are tied to particular languages and local cultures, while pan-Indonesian forms generally use Indonesian and are intended for audiences across regional and ethnic boundaries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee200670-385][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYampolsky1995700-386] These traditions include performance, visual art, ritual, clothing, food, and literature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee200651,_56,_127-387] Modern popular culture includes cinema, television drama, music, celebrity media, and reality shows, along with imported Asian dramas and global formats adapted for Indonesian audiences.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeryanto200832-388][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoutas2008121-389]&#x20;

Indonesia has 16 items on the [UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage Lists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_Lists), including *[wayang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayang)* puppet theatre, *[batik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batik)*, *[angklung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angklung)*, the *[saman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saman_\(dance\))* dance, and *[pencak silat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pencak_silat)*. Since 2024, joint nominations have added *[pantun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantun)*, *[kebaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebaya)*, and *[kolintang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolintang)* to the list.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_–_Intangible_heritage,_cultural_sector"._UNESCO-390]&#x20;

### Visual arts and architecture

Main articles: [Indonesian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_art), [Architecture of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Indonesia), [National costume of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_costume_of_Indonesia), and [Indonesian painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_painting)

A long rectangular batik cotton textile covered with repeating geometric and floral motifs.

An Indonesian [batik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batik)

Indonesian visual arts include textiles, puppetry, masks, carving, and painting.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee200651-391] Textiles are used in rituals and can convey social standing and ethnic affiliation. Colonial rule and nationalism affected the development of modern painting, and tourism and urban art communities became increasingly important later in the 20th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee200660–63-392] Regional painting traditions include Bali's classical *[Kamasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamasan)* tradition, a narrative form often described as *[wayang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayang)* painting.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell2014252-393] Sculptural traditions range from megalithic monuments[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESteimer-Herbet2018vii,_1-31] to Ngaju Dayak *hampatong* figures and *Asmat* artefacts, serving commemorative, protective, aesthetic, or spiritual functions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaiullari2004102–103-394][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Hontheim2010235-395]&#x20;

Indonesian architecture ranges from everyday houses to ritual houses (*[rumah adat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumah_adat)*), whose layouts, posts, roofs, heirlooms, and construction rites can express ancestry, status, and local beliefs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee200683,_90-396] Regional forms include the Javanese *[pendapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pendopo)*, [Dayak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayak_people) longhouses, Minangkabau *[Rumah Gadang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumah_Gadang)*, and Toraja *[Tongkonan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tongkonan)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee200692,_94–95,_98-397] Monumental religious architecture includes [Borobudur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borobudur) and [Prambanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prambanan),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Borobudur_Temple_Compounds"._UNESCO_World_Heritage_Centre-398][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Prambanan_Temple_Compounds"._UNESCO_World_Heritage_Centre-399] while surviving Hindu and Buddhist sculpture from the 8th to 15th centuries includes works in stone, bronze, and gold.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"The_Sculpture_of_Indonesia"._National_Gallery_of_Art,_1990-400]&#x20;

Clothing traditions differ by region and are used in ceremonies, weddings, formal occasions, and as markers of local identity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006142,_147-401] *[Batik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batik)* and *[kebaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebaya)* are widely used in national and formal dress and are especially prominent in Javanese culture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee200665,_143-402] Other regional textile traditions include [Minangkabau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minangkabau) gold-thread textiles and distinctive *[ikat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikat)* traditions among [Batak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batak) and many eastern Indonesian communities. Such textiles are used at formal events, in bridal exchange, ceremonies, and weddings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006144–147-403]&#x20;

### Performing arts

Main articles: [Music of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Indonesia), [Dance in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_in_Indonesia), and [Theatre of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_Indonesia)

Balinese Pendet dancer in a pink-and-gold costume and ornate gold headdress, extending her arms while two similarly dressed dancers perform behind her.

Dancers performing *[Pendet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pendet_dance)*, a traditional [Balinese dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_dance).

Indonesian music and dance include court, village, ritual, and popular forms. *[Gamelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamelan)* traditions are centred especially in Java and Bali, while *[angklung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angklung)* and other bamboo ensembles are used in western Java, Banyumas, and other regions. Drums, gongs, lutes, singing, and music accompanying dance also appear across the archipelago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006189,_192,_194-404] Islamic musical forms include *[gambus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanb%C5%ABs)* and *[qasida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasida)*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarnishRasmussen2011249-405] while popular genres include *[keroncong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kroncong)* and *[dangdut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangdut)*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006195-406] which grew from Melayu music and draws on Indian, Arabic, and Western pop and rock elements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeryanto200813-407]&#x20;

Dance traditions are practiced for different purposes across regions. Mask dances appear in Javanese court drama and Balinese ritual performance, while Dayak mask dances are performed in agricultural rituals and communal ceremonial settings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006196–197-408] Javanese court dance includes forms such as *[bedhaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedhaya)* and *[serimpi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serimpi)*, while other local dances are associated with theatre and music, spirit possession, harvests, temple festivals, cremation ceremonies, and commemorations of the dead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006196–199,_201-409] Contemporary performers also adapt global forms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOh2024407-410][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDahlanAdi202558-411]&#x20;

Traditional Indonesian theatre encompasses storytelling, music, movement, puppetry, masks, acting, and dance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006196,_201–202-412] *[Wayang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayang)* is a prominent puppet-theatre tradition in Java that is also practiced in Bali. Led by a *dalang* and accompanied by *gamelan* music, its repertory frequently reworks episodes from the *[Ramayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramayana)* and *[Mahabharata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahabharata)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELis2019137-413][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006202-414] It has served ritual, moral, comic, political, and public-messaging functions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006202–203-415][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeintraub2004112-416] Other regional traditions combine drama with music, dance, masks, or martial arts,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedanaFoley201684-85-417] and modern theatre groups have used their performances for social and political commentary.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHatley200856-418][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETimmerman201749–50-419]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Indonesian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_literature)

Tan magazine cover with bold black geometric lettering and a stylised human figure.

Cover of the 1937 issue of *[Poedjangga Baroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pujangga_Baru)*, an influential Indonesian literary magazine.

Literary traditions in Indonesia developed in many regional languages and included both oral and written forms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohns197930-420][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiaw20131-421] Oral traditions ranged from storytelling to songs, customs, beliefs, and other community forms, while material could pass between oral and written transmission.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiaw20131–2-422] Traditional written literature included court chronicles, religious and legal writing, narrative prose, and verse. Such forms often served ethical, religious, or dynastic purposes rather than entertainment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohns197931-423]&#x20;

Modern Indonesian literature grew in the early 20th century alongside the expansion of literacy, publishing, and a wider reading public.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohns197931–32-424] *[Balai Pustaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balai_Pustaka)*, established in 1917, distributed books and maintained libraries and agencies across the archipelago, which gave new writers access to wider circulation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohns197932-425] During this period, prose fiction and poetry became more prominent, displacing some older literary forms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuarta202233-426] From 1933, *[Pujangga Baru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pujangga_Baru)* provided another venue for literary and cultural writing, publishing essays, poetry, short stories, and longer prose before the Japanese occupation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohns197933–34-427] Newspapers, magazines, and other publications also carried Indonesian-language prose and poetry during the prewar period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohns197934-428]&#x20;

In the 1970s and 1980s, newspapers and magazines were important outlets for literature, with regular sections carrying poetry, short stories, criticism, and serial fiction.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuarta202283–84-429] From the late 1990s, some forms of literary activity began to emerge in digital media, with websites, mailing lists, forums, and blogs providing spaces for literary communities and for publication.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuarta202294–97-430] Post-1998 literary production has also faced fewer state restrictions,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHellwig2014217-431] with writers able to engage more openly with previously sensitive subjects, such as sexuality and stronger religious expressions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUdasmoroSaktiningrum202211–12-432]&#x20;

### Cinema and mass media

Main articles: [Cinema of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Indonesia) and [Mass media in Indonesia](6532964.md)

Black-and-white studio portrait of a young woman wearing a light headscarf. She rests her chin on folded hands and looking to the side.

[Suzzanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzzanna), an icon of [Indonesian horror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_horror), a prominent genre in the country's cinema.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_Heeren2012137,_147-433]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrianiMurtono2025726–727,_733-434]

Indonesian film production began during the Dutch colonial era with *[Loetoeng Kasaroeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loetoeng_Kasaroeng)* (1926), and a national film industry developed and expanded after independence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Leon_Espena201725,_78-435] Film became a vehicle for nationalist and anti-colonial politics under Sukarno,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Leon_Espena2017139-436] while the New Order regulated and censored cinema.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_Heeren201230-437] The domestic industry of the 1970s and 1980s declined sharply in the early 1990s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanan200856-438] After 1998, independent filmmakers, film communities, festivals, and renewed commercial production helped revive Indonesian cinema.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark200843-439][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParamaditha2017363-440] However, censorship and pressure from non-state actors still limited expression.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark200844-441]&#x20;

During the New Order, newspapers and broadcasters operated under licensing and censorship,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESenHill200053,_84,_88-442] and television was used to advance state-led development and a unified vision of national identity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitley2000331-443] In the *Reformasi* period, the 1999 Press Law prohibited censorship, publication bans, and licensing requirements but journalists and media organisations still face legal cases, political pressure, violence, and other forms of intimidation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWiratraman2014285–286-444]&#x20;

Internet use grew rapidly from the mid-1990s, and websites became another means of distributing news and information.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Share_of_the_population_using_the_Internet_-_Indonesia"._Our_World_in_Data,_2024-445][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYang2010173,_178–179-446] Mainstream media increasingly uses the same reporting across television, print, and online outlets, either through integrated newsrooms or by sharing content between them. News is also shortened or reformatted for websites and social media.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESyarief20244,_7-447]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Indonesian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_cuisine)

Plate of white rice served with beef rendang, vegetables, sambal and other side dishes.

*[Nasi padang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasi_padang)* with *[rendang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rendang)*, *[gulai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulai)*, and vegetables, an example of [Minangkabau cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padang_cuisine)

Indonesian cuisine differs from one region to another in its ingredients, methods, and flavours. Trade and migration brought Indian, Chinese, and Middle Eastern influences over centuries, and Portuguese and other European traders arriving from the 16th century, followed by Dutch colonial rule, introduced further ingredients and cooking practices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006128-448][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWijaya20194-449] Indonesian cuisine also spread abroad, particularly [to the Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_cuisine#Indonesian), where cookbooks and migration introduced Indonesian food to Dutch society by the late 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAriwibowoYayusmanHudaMulyasari202683–84-450]&#x20;

[Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice) is the [staple food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staple_food) in much of the country and is commonly served with meat or fish, vegetables, *sambal*, and other condiments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWijaya20197-451] Chilli, coconut milk, shrimp paste, peanuts, garlic, shallots, and tamarind are widely used in cooking, along with foods such as chicken and seafood.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006131-452] Peanuts are also used to make sauces for dishes such as *[gado-gado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gado-gado)* and *[sate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satay)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006132-453]&#x20;

*[Tempeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tempeh)* and *tahu* ([tofu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tofu)) are widely eaten soybean products. *Tempeh* originated in Java and is also eaten in Bali and elsewhere in Indonesia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006132-453] *Mie* ([noodle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noodle)) and *[nasi goreng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasi_goreng)* are widely eaten in the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006131–132-454] Regional cuisines include [Minangkabau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padang_cuisine) dishes such as *[rendang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rendang)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006132-453] *[Tumpeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumpeng)* is a Javanese ceremonial rice dish served for life-cycle events and other communal observances, where it is associated with gratitude and blessing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJati2014327-455]&#x20;

### Sports

Main articles: [Sports in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Indonesia) and [Indonesian martial arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_martial_arts)

Two barefoot pencak silat practitioners perform on the street before several watching people.

A demonstration of *[pencak silat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pencak_silat)*, a form of martial arts

Popular sports in Indonesia include [football](10568.md), [badminton](3956.md), [weightlifting](37894764.md), and basketball, along with local games and martial arts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006174–177-456] Football is one of the country's most followed sports, with active club supporters and public screenings of matches.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrawiraSuhianto202490-457] [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia_national_football_team) was the first Asian representative to appear at the [FIFA World Cup](11370.md), taking part in the [1938 tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938_FIFA_World_Cup) as the Dutch East Indies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_at_the_1938_FIFA_World_Cup"._FIFA,_2025-458]&#x20;

Indonesia has been particularly successful in international badminton, winning both the [Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Cup) and [Uber Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uber_Cup), the men's and women's world team championships.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"The_Historic_Journey_of_the_Thomas_Cup_&_Uber_Cup"._BWF_Badminton-459] Most of [Indonesia's Olympic medals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia_at_the_Olympics) have come from [badminton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badminton_at_the_Summer_Olympics) and [weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuammar20251101-460] Indonesia first competed at the [Olympics](22576.md) in [1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Summer_Olympics) and has participated in the [Asian Games](39205.md) since the inaugural 1951 edition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Helsinki_1952"._Indonesian_Olympic_Committee-461][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"New_Delhi_1951"._Olympic_Council_of_Asia-462] Jakarta has hosted the Asian Games twice, first in [1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_Asian_Games) and again in [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Asian_Games) with [Palembang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palembang).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Jakarta_1962"._Olympic_Council_of_Asia-463][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Jakarta_-_Palembang_2018"._Olympic_Council_of_Asia-464]&#x20;

Traditional sports and games are featured in ceremonies, harvest events, contests, and public entertainment. Examples include *[sepak takraw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepak_takraw)*, bull racing (*[karapan sapi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karapan_sapi)*) in [Madura](183981.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006175–176-465] and ritual combat traditions such as *caci* in [Flores](11426.md) and *[pasola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasola)* in [Sumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumba).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006174–175-466] *[Pencak silat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pencak_silat)* is an Indonesian martial art[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForshee2006174-467] and was included as an official event at the [2018 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Asian_Games), where Indonesia won all 14 gold medals contested in the sport.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia_Sweeps_Pencak_Silat_Gold_Medals_at_Asian_Games"._Cabinet_Secretariat_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_2018-468]&#x20;

[^cite_note-13]: &#x20;[UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English): [/ˌɪndəˈniːziə, -ʒə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*IN-də-NEE-zee-ə, -⁠zhə*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key) [US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English): [/ˌɪndəˈniːʒə, -ʃə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/93/En-us-Indonesia.ogg/En-us-Indonesia.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/93/En-us-Indonesia.ogg/En-us-Indonesia.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> [*IN-də-NEE-zhə, -⁠shə*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key);<sup>[\[10\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTE"INDONESIA_Definition_&_Meaning"._Dictionary.com-11)</sup><sup>[\[11\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Indonesia"._Merriam-Webster-12)</sup> Indonesian pronunciation: [\[ɪndoˈnesia\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Malay)

[^cite_note-fn1-15]: &#x20;*Republik Indonesia* ([\[reˈpublik ɪndoˈnesia\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Malay) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b8/Id-Indonesia.ogg/Id-Indonesia.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b8/Id-Indonesia.ogg/Id-Indonesia.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) is the most used official name, though the name [Unitary State of the Republic of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Indonesia) (*Negara Kesatuan Republik Indonesia*, NKRI) also appears in some official documents, including the constitution.<sup>[\[12\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTE"1945_Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia"._Ministry_of_Law_and_Human_Rights_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia,_2019-14)</sup>

[^cite_note-97]: &#x20;Estimates range from about 200,000 to one million; at least 500,000 is the widely accepted figure.<sup>[\[91\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECribb2002559-94)</sup><sup>[\[92\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson20183-95)</sup><sup>[\[93\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBevins2020237–238-96)</sup>

[^cite_note-201]: &#x20;The former includes the [Indonesian Democratic Party of Struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Democratic_Party_of_Struggle) (PDI-P), the [Party of the Functional Groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_the_Functional_Groups) (*Golkar*), and the [Great Indonesia Movement Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Indonesia_Movement_Party) (*Gerindra* Party); and the latter includes the centrist [National Awakening Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Awakening_Party) (PKB) and the Islamist [Prosperous Justice Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosperous_Justice_Party) (PKS).

[^cite_note-303]: &#x20;141 people per square kilometre (370 people/mi<sup>2</sup>), per the 2020 national census<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Results_of_the_2020_Population_Census"._Statistics_Indonesia,_20219-7)</sup>

[^cite_note-309]: &#x20;In 2025, Jakarta's population and civil-registration office recorded 11,010,514 residents with official addresses in DKI Jakarta.<sup>[\[301\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTE"Understanding_the_Difference_in_Jakarta_Population_Data:_UN_Data_vs._Population_and_Civil_Registration_Data"._Jakarta_Population_and_Civil_Registration_Office,_2025-307)</sup> The UN figure covers Jakarta together with densely populated communities that form a contiguous urban area around the city centre. The Degree of Urbanization method identifies settlements consistently using population size, density and spatial contiguity thresholds.<sup>[\[302\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTE"World_Urbanization_Prospects_2025:_Summary_of_Results"._United_Nations_Department_of_Economic_and_Social_Affairs,_Population_Division,_20256,_8,_33-308)</sup>

[^cite_note-325]: &#x20;Indonesia is also home to smaller communities of [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Indonesians), [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Indonesians), and [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Indonesians) descent, each with a long-standing presence in the archipelago.<sup>[\[315\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHervandi20111–2-322)</sup><sup>[\[316\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMani2008230–231-323)</sup><sup>[\[317\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl_Qurtuby201748-324)</sup>

[^cite_note-351]: &#x20;[Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) constitute 99% of the Muslim population.<sup>[\[341\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTE"The_Future_of_the_Global_Muslim_Population"._Pew_Research_Center,_2011154-349)</sup> The rest consists of [Shias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_Islam_in_Indonesia) and [Ahmadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya_in_Indonesia), who number between 1–3 million and 200,000–400,000 people, respectively.<sup>[\[342\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshall201889–90-350)</sup>
